                        A manifest of docs/reports
          Two hundred and ninety independent mathematical research packages,
                                 classified by subject

                                      Vladimir Reshetnikov
                                        21 October 2026



1 Scope and conventions
This document catalogues the two hundred and ninety research packages stored under
docs/reports. The collection originally held one hundred and four reports; after two consol-
idations it contributes one hundred and two maintained reports. The other one hundred and
eighty-eight came through ProveIt’s incoming-reports procedure (docs/incoming) — six in
batch 36, one each in batches 40, 41 and 44, two in batch 60, one each in batches 62 and 70,
three in batch 72, sixteen in batch 73, two in batch 74, three in batch 75, two in batch 76,
thirty-four in batch 77, three in batch 78, two in batch 79, two in batch 82, one in batch 83, five
in batch 85, one each in batches 86, 87, 89 and 91, four in batch 100, one in batch 101, three
in batch 102, four each in batches 103, 104 and 105, seven in batch 106, five in batch 107, ten
in batch 108, eight each in batches 109 and 110, five in batch 111, seven in batch 112, eight
in batch 113, five in batch 114, two outside the numbered batches (Report 274 and a two-way
automata manuscript of 7 October), twelve in batch 138 and one in batch 143 — whose archives
remain in the repository’s history. Batches 100–113 are clusters of one session bundle of numbered
Research Reports (arrival 60f54ea06); batch 114 is the seven other archives of two arrivals
of the same day. Later batches also extended existing reports; their entries name the batch.
Batch 80 opened no report here: it extended one report and replaced the programs of three
Parts by corrected code editions; batches 88, 90, 92, 93, 96, 97 and 98 likewise only extended
reports. Batches 81, 84, 94, 95, 99, 115–137 and 139 brought nothing here; the manuscripts of
batches 81 and 84, like part of batches 83, 86, 87, 89, 90, 92, 93, 95, 96 and 97, went to the
surreal-number collection, one batch-85 manuscript, the five of batch 94 and two of batch 95
went to Analysis/Transseries, three manuscripts of batch 97 and the thirteen archives of
batch 99 went to the Fabius drafts tree in Analysis/FabiusFunction, and the one hundred
and six Gowers–Szemerédi manuscripts of batches 115–137 went, by Vladimir’s direction, to
one report beside the Lean development of Gowers’s proof, local-quantitative-refinements
in Combinatorics/Ramsey/Research/GowersSzemeredi. Of the manuscripts of 7 October
that arrived with them but do not concern Gowers’s argument, three came here (the two-way
automata report and Parts of the A373271 and A328716 reports) and the rest went to new
topic directories outside this collection, which docs/incoming lists. The archive of the batch-114
arrivals that continues the A082528 report was held for batch 110, which placed it as that report’s
Part III. Batch 138, the arrivals of 8 October, opened the categories convex-geometry and
probability, placed two further reports among the generating functions and added Parts to
three OEIS reports. Its twelve new reports here are single manuscripts placed without a write,
by Vladimir’s direction of 9 October 2026, so their entries describe the delivered packages. Its
other manuscripts went to local-quantitative-refinements (a hundred and seventh source),
to research directories in Analysis/ErdosSimilarity, Combinatorics/BooleanFunctions
and Combinatorics/Sidorenko, to Analysis/Polylogarithms and to the Fabius drafts tree;
batch 139 went to Analysis/Polylogarithms only. Batch 143 opens set-theory with one
complementary closure-kernel report from three manuscripts; the seeded manuscript is the
base, and a reconciliation README records their overlap and limits without rewriting the
delivered articles. Batch 144 extends the same report with six manuscripts on pure subset closure,
hereditary saturation, certificate coding and machine engineering; it opens no new report and

retains the collection total of 290. Batches 145 and 146 add six more manuscripts on equality
erasure, empty-seed closure, exact grammar compression and a finite-sort obstruction to the
reduced calculus. Batch 147 adds genuine ternary Bottom and a complete Hilbert reference;
batch 148 adds a PA obstruction and complete ground-clause search. Batch 149 adds binding
certificates, shared-template tableaux and a 268-state complete-logic compiler control. Batch 150
adds captured EPSC, two-parameter EPSC2, a protected closure rule and quantified-NAND
tableaux, with separate initializer and compiler controls. The same report now has 178 numbered
sources after batches 205–208. Its written reconciliation covers Sources 1–164 and 167–178;
Sources 165–166 retain their earlier placements and separate pending follow-up. The 241-state
candidate composes two local folds; six later 242-state presentations give the same fold up to
renaming, while their encoding ideas yield a separate joint 1750-bit packet. A subsequent source
rotation enables another fold to an ordinary 240-state candidate, whose exact-table external Lean
consistency endpoint is now checked, together with its internal PA comparison. Batch 172 adds
two independent 239-state savings, and their composition gives an ordinary 238-state candidate,
without a Lean or PA endpoint. Batch 173 delivers that 238-state table five more times and a
further fold to 237 states; batch 175 adds a 236-state machine, which batch 176 repeats twice.
Batch 177 adds a 235-state machine in the original calculus; batch 178 adds machines of 228 to
225 states built on Elliot Glazer’s compact theory K; batch 179 adds a 224-state machine for a
variant of K and a second 225; batch 180 adds a 223-state machine for conditional K; batch 181
adds three more 223s and the intake’s stacked 222-state machine; batch 182 adds an independent
222; batch 183 adds a 221-state machine and four more 222s; batch 184 adds three same-size 221
continuation variants; batch 185 adds a 220-state construction and three continuation alternatives;
batch 186 shares an open equality subtree and adapts the decoder and return routes to obtain 219
states; batch 187 adds three same-size alternatives and two scoped progress companions toward 218;
batch 188 adds a calibrated classical 218-state construction; batches 189–190 retain two research
packages and a distinct contextual 218-state alternative; batch 191 adds a dominated-parameter
203-subtree source and three further complete 218-state variants, with calibrated alternative bases
and scoped failed 217 searches. Batch 192 accepts a 217-state machine using three closed source
rotations and a predecessor-marker state merge. Batch 193 adds residual and negative-equality
calibrations, full countermodels and shorter MP returns, without a smaller accepted machine.
Batch 194 identifies the same 217 under three state labels and retains a separate fixed-label
theory. Batches 195–197 reach an ordinary 215-state construction by combining a partial selector
with command-local field reads. That exact K215 now has compiled Lean and Rocq correctness
proofs; the current work simplifies those proofs. Batches 198–199 add same-size alternatives and
calibrated interfaces. Batch 200 placed Sources 165–166, whose written follow-up is pending;
batches 201–203 placed alternative machines outside this collection without adding numbered
sources. Batch 204 adds the older proof-simplification proposal, a separate GC–ZC215 ordinary
argument and an SB215 return correction. The already checked symmetry refactor is distinguished
from the new zero-offset certificate plan and from uncompiled delivered prototypes. No delivered
executable was run in this intake. The earlier status statements in the entries below are historical;
the current K215 proof status does not formalize the new alternative. Batches 205–208 reconcile
eleven further proof-proposal packets. Exact streaming recovery preserves their original bytes
and historical records; normalized certificates, balanced lookup and relative transport retain
their intake scope. A later phase-six check validates the same K215 endpoint over 187 project
modules and two typed clients, with exact predecessor bindings. Sources 165–166 still await their
written follow-up. Two later 5314ca0e6b/b07002a86b arrivals remain pending. The collection
remains 290 reports. The next paragraphs describe the historical collection before those two
consolidations. On 29 September 2026 the two Lipparini Problem 6.2 reports were unified, and
the finite-height transfinite-word report was integrated with its guarded-block companion. Both
proof routes and all distinct extensions are retained; old directories contain redirects, not extra
catalogue entries. The seven surreal-number reports that this catalogue used to carry were moved
out to a repository of their own, with their history, and are no longer listed here. They arrived as


                                                 2
one hundred and twenty-five ZIP archives, in seven deliveries, which have been unpacked and
grouped into the subject hierarchy described in Section 2. The archives themselves are no longer
kept in the repository.
    One hundred and twenty-five archives yield one hundred and three reports for two different
reasons. Twenty archives duplicated another: sixteen pairs, three three-way clusters and one
four-way cluster. Two more were folded into a neighbour not because they repeated it but because
they shared a subject — the three reports on term counts in the derivatives of power towers are
now one article with a common framework and three parts. That consolidation is editorial, and
the distinction matters: those three were put through the duplicate sweep and cleared it, and
their three theorems are three different theorems. The seventh delivery of eighteen contained
no duplicate at all, which is worth recording: a sweep that finds nothing is evidence about the
collection, not a sweep that failed. Each pair has been merged into one report that proves the
shared theorem once and keeps everything both originals built on top of it. Where the two
reached a result by genuinely different routes, both proofs are kept and marked as alternatives,
with a sentence on what each one buys; the merged shuffle report keeps three independent finite
certifications of one theorem, and the merged report on surreal option graphs kept two different
five-vertex counterexamples for the same reason before it moved out with the other surreal-number
reports. A merged entry below names both of its source archives and says what was doubled.
    Not every pair aimed at one problem is a duplicate. The two Gonshor reports both prove his
product-birthday bound, but on domains that are incomparable in both directions, so neither
theorem contains the other and they are catalogued separately; the same holds for reports
answering different numbered problems of one paper, noted where it occurs.
    Most packages follow the same shape: a typeset article (*.pdf) with its complete LATEX source,
a README.md summarising the result, usually a code/ directory with exact-arithmetic verification
scripts, a data/ or results/ directory holding the recorded output of those scripts, and often
STATUS.md, sources.md or notes/ files auditing the sources consulted and the precise limits of
the claim. The batch 143 closure-kernel reconciliation retains the base LATEX source and selected
supporting files; its README identifies the member articles and PDFs recoverable from the
committed arrival archives.
    Two cautions apply uniformly and are therefore not repeated in the individual entries. First,
these are AI-assisted research drafts: none has been independently refereed or fully checked in a
proof assistant. Standalone checked components are identified with their limited scope. Several
continue a Lean or Rocq project elsewhere in ProveIt; that confers no formal status on them, and
their entries say which project they continue. Second, the descriptions below record what each
report sets out to settle and what it claims to prove. The catalogue does not itself verify those
claims; individual reports retain their review and verification records. Where a report itself flags
a limitation — a prior disproof, an imported theorem, a correction to the source, or an exception
left open — that is noted, because it changes what the report is.
    The naming of the directories is editorial. Several distinct reports attack the same published
problem, and some arrived under colliding archive names, so each directory has been given a name
describing its own angle on the problem; the merged directories usually name their combined
scope. The September 29 consolidations retain the base directory names to preserve existing links.
The original archive names are recorded with every entry.

More than a quarter of the collection consists of counterexamples and corrections rather than
proofs. Ordinal Chomp, open-query games, compositional tree series, the secant numbers, the
decimal-length bound for tetration stabilization, power-tower stabilization, iterated-power digit
stabilization, cluster variables, chromatic coefficients of cycles, products of q-integers, domination
roots, Hardinian array constants, the mixed-base counterexample, recursive exponents, Gregory
sign thresholds and binary substitution discrepancy all refute, or replace with a proved statement,
an assertion published or catalogued elsewhere. Several reports whose main outcome is a proof still
correct the statement they prove: the Somos-8 certificates, the shifted Catalan Hankel polynomials
and the Rueppel determinants record a sign error, a missing one-unit shift and a numbering
ambiguity in their sources, and the independence-system thresholds report refines its target after
finding that the original conjecture had already been disproved by someone else.


3 Ordinals and order types


                                                  4
3.1 Friendly order types
Three reports on Isa Vialard’s friendly order type f (P ), the rank of the tree of open-ended bad
sequences in which every chosen point has an incomparable partner in the current residual. All
three prove the same finite formula; they differ in what they build on top of it, and in how they
prove it — one by a forest-rank monovariant that survives moves deleting a whole upset, one
through a lemma bounding the cut maxima. A fourth report proved the formula by the first
report’s argument almost verbatim and has been merged into it.
1. Friendly Order Types Through Incomparability Components
ordinals-and-order-types/friendly-order-types/
incomparability-components-and-infinite-tails/friendly_order_types.pdf
from friendly_order_type_research (1).zip
The same component formula, together with an exact friendly rank for connected infinite well-
partial-orders: writing o(C) = λ + n with λ the limit part and T (C) the finite set of points with
finite principal upsets. Includes an exhaustive finite verification and a proof audit.
2. Friendly Order Type Through Incomparability Components
ordinals-and-order-types/friendly-order-types/
matroid-rank-and-ordinal-sums/article.pdf
from friendly_order_type_research (3).zip
Identifies |P | − c(P ) with the graphic-matroid rank and the incidence-matrix rank of the incompa-
rability graph. Adds a substitution formula, a finite Cartesian-product formula, and additivity
over arbitrary well-ordered ordinal sums; evaluates every well-partial-order whose incompara-
bility components are finite, and gives countable counterexamples delimiting stronger infinite
extrapolations.
3. Friendly Order Types of Finite Posets and Ordinal Products
ordinals-and-order-types/friendly-order-types/
components-forests-and-ordinal-products/friendly_order_types.pdf
from friendly_order_types_research.zip + friendly_order_type_research.zip
Proves f (P ) = |P | − c(Inc(P )) for a finite poset P , where c counts connected components of the
incomparability graph (isolated vertices included), and classifies the witnesses: every maximum
friendly subset is P minus one minimal root per component, counted by |Min(Ci )|. With
                                                                                  Q

Vialard’s width-transfer theorem this gives ordinal width ω f (P ) for the Dershowitz–Manna multiset
ordering. On the transfinite side it proves f (α1 × · · · × αr × B) = Θ, the natural product, except
that it is the predecessor of Θ when every αi is a successor and B has a greatest element, and
classifies arbitrary finite ordinal boxes. Also: spanning-forest certificates, exact monotonicity
under added comparisons, a bivariate generating function, and explicit four-point factors with
equal (o, h, w, f ) whose products differ. Merged; the Cartesian-product formula is proved twice,
once by corner deletion and once by a chain-grid spanning-subgraph argument.

3.2 Well-quasi-ordered powersets and statures
Four reports on the open questions in Section 6 of Abriola, Halfon, Lopez, Schmitz, Schnoebelen
and Vialard, Measuring well-quasi-ordered finitary powersets (arXiv:2312.14587v2), and on the
corresponding topological invariant.
4. Cofinal strata and ordinal absorption
ordinals-and-order-types/wqo-powersets-and-statures/
cofinal-strata-of-finitary-powersets/article.pdf
from cofinal_strata_ordinal_powersets.zip; batch 42: ProveIt_Ordinal_Heights_Matchings.zip;
batch 36: ProveIt_Nonuniform_Powerset_Heights.zip, ProveIt_Persistent_Frontier_Heights.zip,
persistent_frontier_heights.zip, ProveIt_Ordinal_Heights_Research.zip
For Sα (Q), a finite poset Q with each point replaced by a chain α = ω ρ , proves o(K(Sα (Q))) =
P ρ⊗k
  kω    ck (Q) and h(K(Sα (Q))) = α · height(M (Q)), where ck counts size-k maximal antichains


                                                 5
not dominated by a larger one. The maximal-order-type theorem allows nonuniform pure well-
partial-order fibers and is proved by a finite cofinal-stratification argument. Parts II and III,
merged in batch 36 from four manuscripts that each prove it, supply the nonuniform height
formula the original left open. For positive pure fibers ω ρq , h(K) is the largest ordinary ordinal
sum, along a chain of maximal frontiers, of the largest fiber leaving the frontier at each step
plus the largest fiber of the last frontier; after Cantor-normal-form refinement the same formula
covers nonzero limit fibers. For arbitrary ordinal fibers, finite and successor ones included, the
height is given instead by a finite dynamic program over downsets with fallback weight 1, and the
frontier compression is not valid there. Adds exact point ranks, NP membership of the threshold
problem for the explicit pure-block encoding, and a separate part for well-partial-order fibers with
h(K(Pq )) a positive pure power. Part IV, added in batch 42, shows that for height-two skeletons
with uniform fibers the height is α(νur + 1), with νur the maximum uniquely restricted matching
number, so deciding the height is NP-complete already when every fiber is ω.
5. Intrinsic ordinal approximations for finitary powersets
ordinals-and-order-types/wqo-powersets-and-statures/
intrinsic-ordinal-approximations/article.pdf
from intrinsic_ordinal_approximations.zip
Studies the intrinsic invariant ao (A) = sup{o(B) + 1 : Idl(B) ,→ A}. Proves the universal bound
ao (A) ≤ h(Pf (A)) with equality exactly when every finitely generated downset of A is an algebraic

dcpo, gives the closed formula h(Pf ( i αi )) = sup( i (1 + ai ) + 1), and shows the universal identity
fails on λ + F .

7. Sharp ordinal bounds for Hoare powerspaces
ordinals-and-order-types/wqo-powersets-and-statures/
hoare-powerspace-statures/article.pdf
from hoare_statures_research.zip
For all Noetherian spaces, 1 + ∥X∥ ≤ ∥HX∥ ≤ 2∥X∥ , with witnesses attaining both endpoints at
every ordinal and T1 upper witnesses; the older ω α bound is attainable at infinite input stature α
exactly when ω · α = α; the complete spectrum at ω + n is ω · j(U ) + (j(Q) − j(U )). Extends the
known well-quasi-order results of Abriola et al. to arbitrary Noetherian spaces.

3.3 Transfinite words
One unified report on Harry Altman’s finite-alphabet sharpness question (arXiv:2409.03199v2,
Example 3.15), combining three source manuscripts. The common height-two formula is stated
once. The finite-height induction and the more detailed guarded-tail investigation are retained as
distinct routes.
8. Finite-alphabet transfinite words: exact order types and guarded periodic blocks
ordinals-and-order-types/transfinite-words/order-types-below-omega-squared/article.pdf
from omega2_order_types_proposed_solution.zip + ordinal_words_research.zip +
finite_alphabet_transfinite_words.zip
                        N (P )−1
Claims o(Vk (P )) = ω ω k     for |P | ≥ 2 and each positive finite k, using canonical finite atom
                                                                    n+j(P )−2
posets with Nk+1 = |P | + J(Ik (P )) − 1. At height two this is ω ω           ; empty and singleton
alphabets are explicit exceptions. Retains the selected-tail-family classification, its universal-
only exception, chain/antichain extremality, exact-length strata, and canonical linear-time token
comparison. The guarded proof has both natural-product and elementary amplification routes;


                                                  6
the finite-height proof uses protected separators. Two independent implementations and their
separate audit records are preserved. Neither an all-height union formula nor a higher-cutoff
selected-family classification is claimed.

3.4 Ordinal arithmetic
Three reports on ordinal and infinitary arithmetic, including a consolidation of two independent
proposed solutions to Problem 6.2 of Paolo Lipparini, Monotone infinitary operations on ordinals
(arXiv:2505.00424v2): define and study the least weakly monotone, e-special strictly increasing
operation on countable sequences of ordinals.
9. An Explicit Formula for the Minimal e-Monotone Infinitary Ordinal Sum
ordinals-and-order-types/ordinal-arithmetic/lipparini-minimal-infinitary-sum/article/
explicit_ordinal_sum.pdf
from lipparini_problem_6_2_proposed_solution.zip + ordinal_operation_research.zip
The same three-case formula for the least admissible operation is proved by two retained routes:
profile ranks with arithmetic absorption and finite-product lower bounds, and corrected blocks
with threshold interpolation and finite-part restoration. The latter avoids the product-rank
lemma; both import Lipparini’s characterization of the weaker S. Retains the multiset quotient,
bounded-poset heights, finite-cap ranks with alternative proofs, equality on comparable inputs,
cardinality preservation, and counterexamples to grouping and continuity. The correction is zero, a
finite increment, or one natural summand ω. Both implementations and their separate verification
records are preserved.
10. Jacobsthal Products All at Once
ordinals-and-order-types/ordinal-arithmetic/jacobsthal-products-all-at-once/article.pdf
from Jacobsthal_Products_All_at_Once.zip
Question 2.16 of Altman, Intermediate arithmetic operations on ordinal numbers (MLQ 63 (2017)
228–242), asks for an all-at-once order-theoretic model of finite and infinitary Jacobsthal products,
rather than one built from parenthesized binary multiplication or a limit. Gives a constructive
affirmative answer: a Cantor-normal-form comparison rule on actual tuples or finite-support
functions, invariant under order isomorphisms and extending the componentwise order, whose
definition never evaluates a Jacobsthal product. Adds a restricted maximality characterisation
and a finite-tail reduction. The associativity identity and the binary normal-form formula are
classical and are not claimed here; the construction is not claimed to be preserved by arbitrary
order embeddings.
11. Pairwise Coherence Does Not Guarantee Completion
ordinals-and-order-types/ordinal-arithmetic/
pairwise-coherence-of-infinitary-products/ordinal_product_coherence.pdf
from ordinal_product_coherence.zip
Answers the pairwise-coherence sufficiency question of Lipparini, Noncommutative infinitary
semigroups (arXiv:2605.28413v1, Remark 7.1(b)), negatively. On the two-element group C2 ,
retaining every finite product and declaring every infinite all-e product to be e gives a partial
system satisfying full pairwise regrouping, even for arbitrary partitions, yet with no injective
extension defining all finite and ω-indexed products; two elements is minimal. Adds: for any
monoid M the canonical partial domain P (M ) is coherent, and for directly finite M it completes
exactly when the unit group is trivial; and for every r a system on 3r + 4 elements coherent
through nesting depth r but conflicting at depth r + 1, so no fixed finite depth of the coherence
test characterises completability.




                                                 7
3.5 Games on ordinals

12. An ordinal Chomp transition at 2
ordinals-and-order-types/games-on-ordinals/
ordinal-chomp-transition-at-two/ordinal_chomp_counterexample.pdf
from ordinal_chomp_transition_counterexample.zip + ordinal_chomp_counterexample.zip
For a numerical semigroup S ordered by x ≤S y iff y − x ∈ S, and the ordinal extension ordered by
ordinary ordinal subtraction (so S 2 is the lexicographic, not coordinatewise, square), Chomp on S
is a second-player win while Chomp on S σ is a first-player win for every σ ≥ 2, and ω · 4 + 4 is the
least winning opening. Hence ch(⟨4, 5, 6⟩) = ch(⟨4, 6, 9⟩) = 2, a negative answer to Question 4.6
of Rivero Herrera (arXiv:2504.07317v3) and a refutation of Conjecture 4.1 of v1, already for
three small natural-number generators. The proof reduces the infinite game to a finite spectral
statement via a finite-window recurrence and an exact finite-state certificate, then transfers it
by an explicit response procedure. Merged; the shared ⟨4, 6, 9⟩ certificates were verified to hold
the same 253 rows entry for entry, and the two reports’ poisoned and unpoisoned conventions are
reconciled by an explicit dictionary.
13. Every ordinal as a point-separating game value
ordinals-and-order-types/games-on-ordinals/
point-separating-game-values/ordinal_separation.pdf
from ordinal_separation_research.zip
For every ordinal λ, orders {0, 1}λ lexicographically and gives it the topology of all downward-closed
sets, and the refinement generated by downward-closed sets minus finite sets. The point-separating
game value is exactly λ for both: the first space is compact T0 , the second compact T1 , and for
infinite λ neither is Hausdorff. Taking λ = ω 2 + 1 supplies a compact T1 witness of cardinality
continuum for Problem 1.2 of Chiozini, Csernák and Soukup (arXiv:2510.05754v3), with no CH,
forcing or large-cardinal hypothesis. Hausdorff witnesses are not claimed. This is a different
problem of that paper from the one the open-query games report answers.
14. Open-query games on ordinal spaces
ordinals-and-order-types/games-on-ordinals/open-query-membership-games/article.pdf
from ordinal_membership_games.zip; batch 52: ProveIt_Finite_Output_Open_Query_Games.zip;
batch 56: ProveIt_Filter_Profiles_Open_Query_Complexity.zip; batch 65:
ProveIt_Closure_Word_Duality.zip; batch 68: ProveIt_Universal_Word_Profiles.zip
A negative answer to Problem 1.3 of Chiozini, Csernák and Soukup (arXiv:2510.05754v3): two
copies of [0, ω] each have set-membership number 1, but their topological sum has value 2. The
general theorem computes the invariant for every nonempty Hausdorff space of finite Cantor–
Bendixson height h as ⌈log2 (h + 1)⌉ when the last nonempty derivative is a singleton and
⌈log2 (h + 2)⌉ otherwise. Part II, added in batch 52, replaces the membership bit by one of q
output labels: the optimal depth is ⌈log2 L⌉ for the least number L of ordered layers with open
suffixes; on Hausdorff spaces of finite height L ≤ (q − 1)h + s, sharply, with s the number of
labels on the last derivative; a topological sum of components of depth at most d has depth at
most d + ⌈log2 q⌉, sharply; and computing the optimal depth is polynomial for two labels but
NP-complete for three, even on disjoint chains. At q = 2 it reproduces Part I. Part III, added in
batch 56, answers Part II’s question of when that bound is attained, for spaces whose derived set
is finite and nonempty: a coloring with qc colors needs exactly qc + τ (Gc ) layers, τ the directed
feedback vertex number of a color digraph; the bound is attained exactly under a bin-partition
condition on the partition capacities of the neighborhood filters; and at three labels a sum of three
one-point free-ultrafilter spaces has value 2 while a sum of three convergent sequences has value 3.
Part IV, added in batch 65, drops the separation and finiteness hypotheses for a fixed coloring: a
layer word is admissible exactly when it contains as subsequences all reduced words whose nested
color closure is nonempty. It classifies the finite-depth behaviors, shows that the longest such word
has the length of the least Cantor–Bendixson height of a Hausdorff representative, gives qc + τ (Gc )
layers whenever the second derivative is empty, bounds finite-poset obstructions by mq(q − 1)m−1


                                                  8
points, and proves the product law ρ(X × Y ) = ρ(X) + ρ(Y ) − 1 for that longest length ρ. Part V,
added in batch 68, computes the worst case over colorings at every finite height on locally compact
or sequential (more generally rank-accessible) Hausdorff spaces: maxc L = h(q − 1) + min(q, t) with
t the size of the last nonempty derivative, also when t is infinite, attained by one coloring constant
off a countable set; it adds product and sum laws and an inverse theory of layer and query-depth
spectra. Local data determining an arbitrary fixed coloring at height two, and a characterization
of the spaces attaining that worst case (rank accessibility is sufficient, not necessary), remain
open.

3.6 Order-theoretic invariants without a subcategory
Thirteen reports whose objects sit outside every subcategory above: the minor order on finite
graphs, two on sites of filters on ω, congruences of finite lattices, a translation-invariant ideal, the
lexicographic order of the well-orderings of the reals, measurable prediction in Glazer’s box games
and hat guessing (batches 87, 90, 92 and 96), named elementary embeddings in urelement set
theory (batches 89, 93 and 96), and five reports of batch 114: random bits, finite catalogues and
arbitrary cuts of models of arithmetic, noisy parity on finite and infinite cubes, robust neutral
choice rules, Freiling symmetry from finite blacklists, and bounded-width compression of power
sets. They share a template but no main argument.
15. Ordinal complexity of graph minors
ordinals-and-order-types/graph-minor-ordinals/graph_minor_ordinals.pdf
from graph_minor_ordinals.zip
For finite simple graphs of maximum degree at most two under minor containment, computes
maximal order type, ordinal antichain-tree width and descending-tree height: o(D) = w(D) = ω ω·2
and h(D) = ω, with exact values ω b+k and ω b+k−1 for the subfamilies bounded by path size b
and cyclic-component count k. The general theorem is that a wqo C of connected graphs with
o(C) = α < ε0 has finite disjoint-union closure of type ω α , proved by a component-splitting
argument, since minor containment is not multiset embedding. A corollary is that continuity fails
for increasing unions of minor-closed classes. The report is explicit that it does not compute the
maximal order type of the minor order on all finite graphs and settles no named conjecture.
16. A two-valued obstruction to gluing Fréchet powers
ordinals-and-order-types/frechet-two-valued-obstruction/article.pdf
from frechet_two_valued_obstruction.zip
For the presheaf F 7→ Aω /∼F on proper filters on ω containing the cofinite filter, with the dense
Grothendieck topology, proves in ZF that it is a sheaf if and only if |A| ≤ 1. The counterexample is
an explicit matching family of constant sections using two values, on a covering sieve with countably
many generators each of countable base; the distinction between the number of sieve generators
and the size of the full sieve is essential to it. The question is Massas, A Semi-Constructive
Approach to the Hyperreal Line (2023), p. 517 after Fact 5.10, repeated in the 2024 dissertation
Duality and Infinity, p. 249.
17. Bounded-character Fréchet filter sites
ordinals-and-order-types/frechet-bounded-character-sites/article.pdf
from filter_complexity_research.zip, Frechet_Cardinal_Boundaries.zip
Continues the previous entry’s non-claim about other filter sites. For the site Pκ of free filters on
ω generated by fewer than κ sets modulo the cofinite filter, represents the Booleanization Cκ as a
regular-open algebra on the free ultrafilters and proves that P (ω)/Fin is order dense in it exactly
for κ ≤ p; above p no complete Boolean homomorphism preserving the modulo-finite events exists
in either direction. The atoms are the ultrafilters of character below κ, so Cκ is atomless exactly
for κ ≤ u; h is the first size of a family of events that cannot be decided jointly on a dense set of
regions. Computes the associated sheaf by Boolean mixtures, with full Boolean-valued transfer,
                                                                         c
countable saturation of ultrafilter specializations, and sizes c, 2c , 22 for the raw power and the two


                                                   9
sheafifications. A three-stage example uses Millán’s forcing model as an imported input. Merged
from two manuscripts whose indexings differ by a successor shift; the second one’s closure-based
results are stated at successor stages only.
18. A sharp cover bound for congruences of finite lattices
ordinals-and-order-types/lattice-congruence-cover-bound/sharp_cover_bound.pdf
from lattice_cover_bound_research.zip
If a finite n-element lattice has an element with at least k ≥ 3 upper covers then |Con(L)| ≤ 2 n−k−1 ,
with equality exactly for the glued sums Cs ⊕ Mk ⊕ Ct . This replaces the multicolour Ramsey
threshold Rk−1 (k) of the source’s Lemma 4.4 (arXiv:2603.11454) by k itself. Adds a sharp
nonextremal gap, a fan-label ideal refinement, and a bound on the size of Czédli’s skeleton in
terms of congruence density. All congruence machinery, including Freese’s maximum, is proved
from scratch. The target is an informal suggestion in the source, not a numbered conjecture.

ordinals-and-order-types/non-baire-translation-invariant-ideal/article.pdf

An affirmative construction for Question 1 of Filipów and Tryba, Densities for sets of natural

20. Lexicographic orders of the well-orderings of the reals
ordinals-and-order-types/lexicographic-well-orderings-of-reals/article.pdf
from batch 76: lexicographic_well_orderings_of_reals.zip + Lexicographic_Well_Orderings_of_Reals
(1).zip + lexicographic_well_orderings_research.zip
With κ = 2ℵ0 and W the order of all well-orderings of R, as enumerations compared at their
first unequal real entry, proves in ZFC that |W| = 2κ , that every linear order of size at most
κ embeds, even into each fixed-type slice, and that an ordinal embeds exactly when it is below
κ+ ; yet the binary lexicographic coding length and the real representation rank of W are both
κ+ , so W embeds into no bounded union of strata, in particular not into the slice Wκ , which
is equimorphic with the cube 2κ in lexicographic order and with the Kanovei–Shelah ultrafilter
                                                                                                   γ
index. Also: each stratum Wλ+n , λ a limit of cardinality κ, is equimorphic with 2λ × n!; Wlex
is equimorphic with W exactly for 0 < γ < κ+ ; adjacency and the n! blocks; the exact point-
character and gap spectra (every gap has a countable side; exactly 2κ gaps), a (cf κ, cf κ) gap
of Wκ , a dense finite-condensation quotient equimorphic with W, bounded surreal sign orders
and an ultrafilter index built from well-orderings. The cube hierarchy re-proves a corollary of
point-separating-game-values, cited first; real-power strictness is Kuhlmann’s theorem; no
priority is claimed, and no intrinsic embedding criterion or classification of the gaps of the longer
strata. Merged from three independent manuscripts proving the same core: each shared theorem
printed once, with the other arguments as second routes. Each answers a question of another:
the ZFC non-embedding into Wκ , which one had only under 2<κ < 2κ , and the cut and character
spectra and the quotient another had asked about.
21. Countable information, uncountably many boxes: measurable prediction, hat-
guessing surplus, adaptive extensions, query and randomness thresholds
ordinals-and-order-types/measurable-box-games/article.pdf
from batch 87: glazer_box_games_research.zip (Part I); batch 90: hat_guessing_research.zip
(Part II); batch 92: adaptive_box_games_research.zip (Part III),
hat_query_thresholds_package.zip (base) + hat_inspection_frontier.zip (Part IV); batch 96:
hat_randomness_frontier.zip (Part V)
Part I takes up the question that ends Section 5 of Glazer’s A choiceless box game paradox
(arXiv:2211.10474): does ZF prove that three players facing continuum many boxes of bits can
guarantee that at most one of them guesses wrong? It separates a measurable choice of box
and guess, a measurable success event and actual executability, and answers the question for


                                                  10
the first class only. For a standard Borel set of boxes, alphabet size q and m blind output
maps measurable for the cylinder σ-algebra, the guaranteed number of correct guesses is exactly
⌊m/q⌋, without assuming the success events measurable; a countable team has a fair simultaneous
measure extension; measurable success forces a countable target range; a continuous selector
has a success event of inner measure 0 and outer measure 1. Blind binary maps on n boxes are
labelled perfect matchings of the cube, executable maps exactly the recursively sliceable ones
(the two classes first differ at four boxes); their number Fn = 1, 2, 9, 232, 206065, . . . satisfies an
                                                            n
exact recurrence and the all-orders expansion Fn = eγ2 n−1 (1 − 2n    3     17
                                                                         + 4n     12
                                                                              2 − n3 + O(n
                                                                                             −4 )) with

a growth constant γ ≈ 0.438158, not Euler’s; a doubly exponential rarity bound, information
inequalities, an oracle extraction of finite losing prefixes, and a Busy Beaver comparison that is
conditional on an encoding and uses a program-length running-time function, not Rado’s score
function. Neither counting sequence was in the OEIS on 3 October 2026. Part II (batch 90)
answers both questions of Eldredge’s Remark 6.8 (arXiv:2508.02828) on the surplus Dn = Sn − n/2
of correct guesses in the infinite binary hat game: Hamming-syndrome blocks give a computable
strategy with Dn = log2 n + O(1) almost surely, and for every positive g = o(n) a continuous
strategy with Dn /g(n) → ∞ almost surely, so no sublinear envelope is universal; the coding
mechanism is credited to Ebert–Merkle–Vollmer. Its finite corollary settles Part I’s question Q9
for N = 2a(2r − 1) binary players and threshold a2r : the optimum is exactly 1 − 2−r , over
legal trees and blind maps alike. Part III (batch 92) classifies every extension of the product
measure to the success events of adaptive players by measurable densities on a random set of
feasible success patterns, which answers Part I’s question Q5, with an atomic-mass law, a Hall
polytope of joint laws and kernels for countable teams; the minimax ⌊m/q⌋ holds for full Borel
and universally measurable blind outputs, two of the three clauses of Q2, while for Baire-property
outputs an invariant comeagre null set blocks the averaging proof and that clause stays open.
Part IV (batch 92) merges two independent manuscripts that answer Part II’s question on the
cost of inspecting hats by the same construction: a common bound on adaptive queries makes
divergence of the surplus null and meagre, and the exact expected-query threshold for deterministic
trees is 2, attained by a computable strategy with fewer than two expected queries per player.
The second source adds a lower bound for every nondecreasing cost, a central limit theorem
and a classification at minimum depth that settles Q9 for (m(2r − 1), 2, m(2r − 1)) at threshold
m2r−1 for every m ≥ 1. Two sentences calling the remaining cases of Q9 open are retained and
refuted: the batch-90 one by Part I’s own three-box team (Remark 15.1), and the batch-92 one on
every q ≥ 3 case above ⌊m/q⌋ by zero-query constant guesses at a shared target, which attain
m/(qt) whenever t divides m (Remark 15.2). Part V (batch 96) answers the almost-sure clauses of
Part IV’s question 57.2, on that cost when random bits are free: with independent private coins, or
with a public seed that is almost surely finitely valued, the admissible uniform expected inspection
caps are (1, ∞), the endpoint excluded (at cap 1 private coins succeed with probability at most
1 − 1/5184), whereas one shared random integer of countably infinite support attains cap 1 with
every individual expected cost below 1 and arbitrarily small entropy. A prescribed public seed
with values in a standard Borel space permits cap 1 iff it is not almost surely finitely valued, and
if the cap must hold conditionally on the public seed the caps are (1, ∞) with private coins and
[2, ∞) without. The tools are a sign bound by projection onto the players who inspect nothing,
owner-indexed Walsh accounting and a stopped supermartingale lemma; Part IV’s thresholds
and Bonami’s lemma are re-proved with credit. The private positive-probability threshold at
cap 1 stays open. The manuscript’s hypothesis “finite positive-probability support” makes its
finite-public theorem false as worded, since a seed with one atom and a diffuse part permits
the endpoint (Remark 75.6); the write prints the hypothesis as probability-one concentration on
finitely many values, with the delivered wording on record, corrects the abstract’s “countably
supported” seed to countably infinite support, and moves three unproved statements, among them
the unsupported statement that its proofs were independently reviewed, to the questions. The
publication review 47ab39154 then refuted two sentences of the write itself, retained in Remark 75.9:
the guide’s extension of the gap 1/5184 to finite public seeds (a two-valued public gate succeeds


                                                  11
with probability at least 9999/10000 below cap 1) and an equality in the proof of Corollary 75.7,
now an inequality; neither theorem changes. Glazer’s question itself is not answered; Part I’s
Q1 is not the “Question 1” of the surreal report polish-models-of-omnific-arithmetic. The
Hilbert’s-tenth programme reviewed Part I’s computability claims (f15962acd, not its probability,
enumeration or asymptotic claims), routed Part II’s archive from its READMEs only (bcc1a4438)
and reviewed the publication of Parts III–IV within a stated scope (42b49fdbd, the source of
Remark 15.2); for Part V it read the manuscript in two bounded passes (18825ee16, 1eab36bb3,
both reading the seed hypothesis as atomic), authenticated the placement (608e06daa), confirmed
the hypothesis correction independently (c52f7d055) and reviewed the publication (47ab39154).
No priority; not formalized.
22. Naming elementary embeddings: atom components, Replacement, reflection,
commuting actions and relations among injections
ordinals-and-order-types/naming-elementary-embeddings/article.pdf
from batch 89: Naming_Elementary_Embeddings_Research.zip (Part I); batch 93:
commuting_actions_replacement.zip (Part II); batch 96: atom_actions_research.zip (base) +
commuting_injections_research.zip (Part III)
In Yao’s kernel-ideal models Mκ (A) of urelement set theory with Replacement and choice (objects
whose transitive closures involve fewer than κ atoms), every injection f of the atoms lifts to a
canonical amenable elementary self-embedding jf fixing every pure set, and the initial elementary
self-embeddings are exactly these lifts. When finitely many lifts are named, so that the schemes
range over formulas containing them, Replacement holds exactly when the small type blocks of
the atom graph have fewer than κ atoms (for κ = ω: finite components and a finite small part).
Hence every one-name expansion keeps Replacement iff cf κ > ω, every expansion by two or more
names iff cf κ > 2ℵ0 , and at ℵ2 universal two-name preservation is equivalent, externally, to CH (a
characterization, not a proof or refutation of CH). Collection fails at limit cutoffs already for ∆0
formulas of the base language and holds in full exactly at an uncountable successor κ with fewer
than κ component types; full transitive reflection has the same criterion; two individually safe
automorphisms can be jointly unsafe. The kernel-ideal construction is Yao’s and the lifting idea is
not claimed new. The same Collection spectrum is proved independently in Part VI of the surreal
report birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets, whose Part VIII (batch 90) contains the case
κ = ω of the criterion for finitely many named permutations, proved independently. Part II
(batch 93) names commuting maps: every tuple of commuting permutations keeps expanded
Replacement iff cf κ > ω, which answers Part I’s question on commuting automorphisms in full;
with commuting injections added the threshold is cf κ > ω for at most one injection and cf κ > 2ℵ0
for two or more, and two commuting proper injections, each harmless alone, can jointly destroy
Replacement; for commuting permutations the atom kernel of definable closure is computed exactly
(Part I’s Question 10.1 in that case); for a finitely generated group named by generators every
action keeps Replacement iff the group is finite (κ = ω) or cf κ exceeds its number of conjugacy
                                                                  L
classes of subgroups; one uniform evaluator for an action of ω C2 with orbits of at most two
atoms destroys Replacement whenever cf κ ≤ 2ℵ0 while separate names keep it; and set forcing that
keeps κ a cardinal preserves the verdict for a fixed action (the fixed-action part of Question 10.10).
Part VIII of the surreal report is credited as prior at κ = ω; one imported group-existence
theorem (Ivanov–Ol’shanskii, read through Smith) is not proved, and its primary check is an open
question. Part III (batch 96) merges two independent manuscripts on relations among named
injections, which answer Part II’s Question 23.3 by the same main theorem: for a finitely generated
commutative monoid acting by injections, with G the group completion of its cancellative image
and d the rank of G modulo the units, the connected labelled types number |Sub(G)| if G is finite,
ℵ0 if G is infinite and d ≤ 1, and 2ℵ0 (with a rigid Cantor family) if d ≥ 2; d is decidable from a
finite commutative presentation; every such action keeps Replacement for all κ, iff cf κ > ℵ0 , iff
cf κ > 2ℵ0 in the three cases; the relation f m = g n is enumerated by necklace counts, with rational
Catalan numbers in the coprime case; and forcing adds a connected type for d ≥ 2 iff it adds a real.
The base adds pure-real rooted codes of components, a centralizer-orbit count that generalizes a


                                                 12
lemma of Part VIII of the surreal report to injections and infinite components, and the exact atom
kernel of the objects definable from given atoms and finitely many pure reals, for any finite tuple of
injections; so Part II’s Questions 23.1 and 23.2 and Part I’s Question 10.1 are answered only with
pure real parameters, and ordinary definability stays open. Results proved by both sources are
printed once, with the second proof as a marked route. Re-scoped at the write: the second source’s
“commutative answer” to Question 23.5 decides only the commutative-monoid analogue, and the
base’s countability argument for the parameter-free definable kernel is metatheoretic, kept as prose
and asked as Question 37.17. The Hilbert’s-tenth research tree read the second manuscript in full
and selected interfaces of the base before placement (18825ee16), authenticated the placement
(b16786caf) and reviewed the write within a bounded scope without finding a mathematical
defect (6d78c238d); 6d06f8952 corrected the README’s sentence that no review of Part III
existed, retaining it as a review remark. It continues the formal projects SetTheory/ZF and
SetTheory/BoundedConsistency semantically and proposes a formalization route; their formula
type has no atom predicate, and nothing here is formalized. Unrefereed; priority not established.
23. Random bits, finite catalogs and arbitrary cuts of arithmetic: exact noise
thresholds, Boolean universality and the strength of induction
ordinals-and-order-types/random-bits-arithmetic-cuts/article.pdf
from batch 114:   Random_Bits_Arithmetic_Cuts.zip
For a countable model M of PA and an external random predicate P ⊆ M , the coding cut
Cut(P ) is the initial segment of those a for which P ∩ [0, a) has an internal binary code. For
pairwise-independent bits with biases pt , modal bit b and εt = min(pt , 1 − pt ), almost surely
Cut(P ) = Cut(b) ∩ {a : t<a εt < ∞} (external sums). Fair bits give the standard cut, announced
                         P

by Elliot Glazer (2023, for PA “or even Q”); the PA case is reconstructed with the report’s
own formula, and neither the Q case nor uncountable models are claimed. Every cut is the
almost-sure coding cut of a full-support nonatomic product law; for independent arrays a family
of conjunction cuts is realizable iff it is inclusion monotone, and every finitary Boolean observable
has the intersection of the cuts of its minimal sensitive supports. A proper cut refutes IΣ1 (P );
                                                                               P
Cut(P ) = M iff (M, P ) satisfies I∆0 (P, E); for zero-modal noise, W = a pa separates three
regimes (full induction; I∆0 (P, E) without IΣ1 (P ); neither). Also category and Baire-class results,
the sharp pairwise atom bound 1/(N +1) or 1/(N +2) (Benjamini–Gurel-Gurevich–Peled, credited),
and finite catalogs: bounded budgets iff εi < ∞, an exact Hamming-layer formula, the normal
                                              P

transition (Kontoyiannis–Verdú, Chen–Effros–Kostina, credited) and a Poisson staircase for
pN = λ/N . The write proves Remark 13.1: Cut(P ) = M makes P a class, hence not strongly
CP-generic (Abdul-Quader–Schmerl), partly settling Question 5; all fourteen questions stay
open and priority is not established. Only the passing remark that PA has no finite models
is formalized (Logic/PeanoArithmetic/NoFiniteModel; the independent check added same-
named copies in an unimported optimization file); beta coding and a definable-cut induction
failure of Logic/PeanoArithmetic/NotFinitelyAxiomatizable are ingredients, and the route
of Section 11 is a proposal. Unrefereed; not formalized.
24. Noisy parity on finite and infinite cubes: exact coloring, inspection budgets,
asymptotic laws and measurable choice
ordinals-and-order-types/noisy-parity-cubes/article.pdf
from batch 114:   Noisy_Parity_Finite_and_Infinite_Cubes.zip
A uniform configuration X of finitely or countably many bits is compared with a copy Y in which
one coordinate, drawn with ordered weights p1 ≥ p2 ≥ · · · , is flipped and every bit is flipped
independently with probability η; one measurable label f succeeds when f (X) ̸= f (Y ). For
0 < η < 1/2 the optimum is maxk [1 + (1 − 2η)k (2Pk − 1)]/2, Pk = p1 + · · · + pk , attained by a
prefix parity on K coordinates, K finite also on the countable cube, with at most one adjacent tie
and a first-sign-change certificate; under strictly decreasing weights the optima are exactly the
signed prefix parities, with a quantitative stability gap. The exact expected-inspection frontier
is linear between the points (k, wk ), k ≤ K, and constant beyond, attained by shared mixtures


                                                 13
of adjacent prefix parities; a spectral simulation replaces every measurable rule by a random
parity χS with Pr(S = T ) = fˆ(T )2 , at mean cost equal to the total influence. At zero noise
the measurable supremum 1 is not attained. A total parity label exists under AC, and over ZF
exactly under a restricted choice of Eev -classes; it is measurable for a biased product measure iff
    min(qi , 1 − qi ) < ∞; for summable heterogeneous noise the optimization over all subsets of N is
P

compact and, in ZFC, a label with a measurable success event attains its value. Small-noise laws
for uniform weights (saturation at ηn = 1), geometric weights (a logarithmically periodic term),
regularly varying and Zipf weights. The write adds, with proofs, Remark 5.2 (no deterministic
decision tree attains the frontier at a non-integer budget 0 < c < K) and Remark 6.4 (on the
countable cube the stability gap tends to 0 as η → 0). Two classical lemmas of Part IV of
measurable-box-games (total influence at most the expected number of inspections; the Walsh
degree of a depth-d tree) are re-proved and printed as second routes. The machinery is classical
(O’Donnell; Geschke–Lubarsky–Rahn and Serafin; Conley–Miller), priority is not established, and
fifteen further questions stay open. The independent check found nothing to correct. Unrefereed;
not formalized.
25. Robust choices that never settle: an exact finite–infinite puzzle about resilience,
choice and randomness
ordinals-and-order-types/robust-neutral-choice/article.pdf
from batch 114:   robust_choices.zip
A neutral rule f : {−1, 1}n → {−1, 1} (f (−x) = −f (x)) is robust to r edits at x when no r
coordinate changes flip its output. The largest probability of robustness is 21−d j≤(d−1)/2−r dj ,
                                                                                   P

d the largest odd integer ≤ n, attained for every r at once by majority of d voters, so an even
committee does no better than one fewer member: a corollary of Kleitman’s diameter theorem,
credited to Kleitman and to Huang–Klurman–Pohoata, with the reduction and the constructions
proved. A sharp prefix inequality bounds ∥E[f | Fm ]∥∞ by 2m /V (m, r) times the vulnerability, V
the Hamming-ball volume, with the best dimension-free constant. If one-edit vulnerability tends
to 0 the decisions are mixing and change sign infinitely often on a conull comeagre set; a Hamming-
code construction separates one-edit from two-edit resilience; a subsequence makes the decision
map a continuous open surjection with density within e±η of fair Bernoulli measure. In ZFC a
neutral rule invariant under finite changes exists on all infinite streams, none is measurable and
neither fibre has the Baire property; for a product of Bernoulli(pi ) laws a completed-measurable
one exists iff (pi − 1/2)2 = ∞, a deduction from Kakutani’s dichotomy. The write proves
                P

Proposition 11.1 (for several labels and a group with no global fixed label the fibres are not all
measurable and not all Baire), the regularity half of Question 6; re-scopes Questions 1 and 8
(existence of the rule is equivalent over ZF to choosing one of each complementary pair of
E0 -classes); and in Remark 10.1 derives the category half of the ZFC theorem from proved
declarations of SetTheory/Cardinals/Cardinals/Countable/GenericErgodicity.lean, given
complementation as a homeomorphism, which the library does not define. The finite statements
were checked exhaustively in dimensions 1–5 (65,814 neutral rules); ten questions stay open; no
priority is claimed. The independent check found nothing to correct. Unrefereed; not formalized.

Studies maps f : P(X) → X whose fibers contain no antichain of r + 1 sets, the property
SCr (X), extending    Glazer’s r = 1 theorem (his accepted MathOverflow answer, imported). With
          n 
Wn = ⌊n/2⌋    , a finite poset of width w needs exactly ⌈w/r⌉ labels (Dilworth), so qr (n) = ⌈Wn /r⌉,

Over ZF an infinite set with SCr is Dedekind-infinite, with an injection of N definable from f and

X is finite, nonempty and W|X| ≤ r|X|. The ZF case r ≥ 2 is posed as open, with the profile
of a counterexample. Wrong as printed, and kept with a numbered Remark 9.10 of the write:
the definition of an antichain code does not require injectivity, and then the antichain-transfer
lemma and theorem are false (X = {0, 1, 2}, r = 1, Θ constantly ∅, where SC1 (X) holds);
requiring injectivity repairs them, and the only code the source constructs is injective, so the ZFC
classification stands, as the independent check confirmed. That code is Pruss’s for r = 1 in the
same MathOverflow thread, credited by the write. The source’s statement that ProveIt already
has finite-poset and Boolean-lattice work is scoped: no Lean or Rocq file treats Dilworth, Sperner
or poset width. Seventeen further items (F1–F17) are stated with what is missing; priority is not
established. Unrefereed; not formalized.


4 Automata and formal languages
Nine reports on state complexity, synchronization, language hierarchies, crossover closures, additive
complexity, the asymptotic enumeration of automata and size lower bounds for two-way automata.
Three are merges: the shuffle bound arrived four times, the DFAO reversal bound twice, and the
last report joins a manuscript on accessible automata with one on strongly connected automata
that adapts its method.
28. The six-state shuffle bound
automata-and-formal-languages/shuffle-six-state-bound/shuffle_six_state.pdf
from shuffle_six_state_research (1).zip + shuffle_six_state_research.zip +
Shuffle_Six_State_Theorem.zip + six_state_shuffle_research.zip
For complete DFAs with m, n ≥ 2 and min(m, n) ≤ 6, the worst-case deterministic state complexity
of the shuffle is F (m, n) = 2mn−1 + 2(m−1)(n−1) (2m−1 − 1)(2n−1 − 1), over a finite alphabet allowed
to depend on m and n; the unrestricted conjecture stays open. The route is a reachability theorem
in the universal shuffle automaton — a subset is reachable exactly when it meets the initial row
and the initial column — proved by induction on (m + n, |S|) down to a finite certificate obligation.


                                                    15
Merged from four reports. Two shipped the same 10,642 witnesses; a third supplies an independent
database of 10,619 witnesses sharing no witness with them, so the finite step is certified twice over;
a fourth adds a fifth analytic reduction that cuts the obligation to 453 certificates and replaces the
solver search with a deterministic enumeration. All three routes are kept.
29. A coloring obstruction to maximal binary DFAO reversal
automata-and-formal-languages/dfao-reversal-coloring-obstruction/article.pdf
from dfao_reversal_research.zip + binary_dfao_reversal_research.zip; batch 39:
ProveIt_Three_Output_Reversal.zip; batches 40–41: ProveIt_DFAO_Eventual_Optimality.zip,
ProveIt_Binary_DFAO_Reversal.zip, ProveIt_Binary_Reversal_Exactness.zip; batch 43:
ProveIt_Four_Output_Reversal.zip, ProveIt_Four_Output_Reversal (1).zip; batch 67:
ProveIt_Near_Linear_DFAO_Reversal.zip, ProveIt_DFAO_Spectral_Cancellation.zip
For transformations a, b on an n-element set and an output map to k labels, proves |τ ⟨a, b⟩| ≤
k n − k! + g(k) < k n for 3 ≤ k ≤ n, with g(k) the largest order of a permutation of k symbols
— the binary-alphabet nonattainment question of Davies, arXiv:1705.07150v2, Section 5. The
mechanism is a collision-orbit graph, a rightmost singular factorization, and a cyclic relabeling
bound. Adds an abelian-permutation extension, a structural universal bound matching the
published lower construction on all 372 pairs with 7 ≤ n ≤ 30, and polynomial-time missing-
coloring certificates. Merged; the two originals ran the same three-case argument, and both a
qualitative short proof and the counting proof are kept. Part II, added in batch 39, settles three
outputs exactly: R2 (n, 3) = 3n −3(2A +2B −2)+B for every n ≥ 7, with (A, B) the closest coprime
split of n, together with rigidity of the extremizers; the formula matches all twenty-four k = 3 rows
of the finite table. Part III, merged in batches 40–41 from three manuscripts, settles every k ≥ 4
past an explicit threshold: R2 (n, k) = k n − Bipk (an , bn ) + an bn once n ≥ N (k) ≤ 3k 4 , and for all
n ≥ max(7, k + 1) when k = 4, . . . , 7 (computer-assisted below N (k)), with the lower construction
imported from Davies; a non-effective eventual route for every k ≥ 3, 919 finite certificates and a
second proof of the k = 3 formula are kept. Explicit exactness for k ≥ 8 below min(N (k), T (k)),
with T (k) the threshold of Part V, stays open. Part IV, merged in batch 43 from two manuscripts
on k = 4, re-derives that case with an analytic threshold n ≥ 26 (against 212), and adds stability,
the missing-orbit inventory, the extremal output maps and a minimal order-15 recurrence. Part V,
added in batch 67, proves the formula for every k ≥ 4 once n ≥ T (k) = 4k log k + O(k) instead of
n ≥ 3k 4 (so for all k up to cn/ log n, c < 1/4), with a theta-function limit of the optimal deficit
when n/k 2 tends to a constant and a Gaussian law in between. Part VI, added in batch 67,
determines the minimal recurrences of the explicit witness deficit for every fixed k: order 9 at
three outputs (against Part II’s 12), a product-12 cancellation on the odd residues but not in
the full sequence at nineteen outputs, the only product-12 cancellation at any k, and explicit tail
numerators.
30. Synchronizing Dżyga’s automata by a cyclic retract
automata-and-formal-languages/dzyga-synchronizing-retract/article.pdf
from Dzyga_Automata_Cyclic_Retract_Research.zip
For the Figure-3-complete automaton Ak of Conjecture 8 in Szykuła’s 2026 survey, exhibits an
explicit word Rk = p(ecr−1 )r−2 e with r = k + 4 that sends every state to q2 for every k ≥ 1, of
expanded length 8k 2 +54k+92 = 89 n2 + 22
                                        3 n+16 for n = 3k+6. The proof is a cyclic retraction with
two projected reflections whose discrepancy supplies one adjacent merger, and does not depend
on the finite computations; shortening the reflected tail by one state gives a nonsynchronizing
automaton with a dihedral permutation quotient. This settles the synchronization clause of the
stated model, not the numerical threshold conjecture, and the universal lower bound τ (q0 ) ≥ 4k + 8
is not proved.
31. Gaps in bounded-shuffle hierarchies
automata-and-formal-languages/insertion-degree-spectra/article.pdf
from shuffle_spectra_research.zip; batch 41:     ProveIt_Binary_Insertion_Spectra.zip; batch 66:
ProveIt_Sharp_Insertion_Isolation.zip
A counterexample to the insertion-degree No-Gap Conjecture of Hughes, arXiv:2608.27755v1,

                                                   16
Section 11, followed by positive results: every finite set of positive integers containing 1 is the
insertion-degree spectrum of two nonempty finite languages over a suitable alphabet, and any
prescribed finite list of degrees above 1 is realizable with every other output of degree 1. A ternary
pair has spectrum {1, 3} with sole difficult output abcba, whose length 5 is the minimum possible
for a gap witness; a binary pair has spectrum {1, 2, 4} while all 18 singleton source-pair spectra
are initial intervals, so source-pair ambiguity can erase a degree. The separate iteration-depth
no-gap conjecture is proved for arbitrary languages. Part II, added in batch 41, fixes the alphabet:
every finite set containing 1 is the spectrum of finite binary languages with the single inserted
word am , so two letters always suffice; its m2 length bound holds in its full-coverage class only.
Part III, added in batch 66, determines that class exactly: an isolated degree-m output with g
gaps between its a’s (the two end gaps included) and T letters a exists exactly when g ≥ m and
T ≥ max(g(m − 1) + 1, τm ), with τ3 = 12 and τm = 5m − 2 for m ≥ 4, so the minimum lengths
for m = 3, 4, 5 are 14, 21 and 27. It characterizes the extremal centers and the mandatory targets,
and at m = 3 classifies the shortest target languages: 39 = 19,683 of them, of minimum size 37,
attained by 512.
32. Constrained crossover closure
automata-and-formal-languages/constrained-crossover-closure/article.pdf
from ProveIt_Constrained_Crossover.zip (batch 44); batch 66: ProveIt_Crossover_Stabilization.zip,
ProveIt_Crossover_Classification.zip; batch 71: quadratic-finite-rank-package.zip; batch 73:
rational-rank-slopes-package.zip, dfa-crossover-package.zip, common-path-rank-package.zip (the
earlier editions dfa-crossover-package (1).zip and common-path-rank-package (1).zip superseded,
not printed)
Answers the question Hughes (arXiv:2608.27755v1, Section 4) leaves open on the complexity
of deciding L ⊗c L = L for the constrained crossover of a context-free language: it is co-r.e.-
complete. Adds an aligned-rank no-gap theorem for iterated crossover and effectivity results
in which Presburger quantifier elimination is used only as an algorithmic step. Related to, but
using nothing from, the insertion-degree report above. Part II, added in batch 66, shows finite
stabilization of regular seeds PSPACE-complete for NFA input, already over two letters; the
upper bound also covers DFA input, improving Part I’s EXPSPACE bound (Part VI settles
DFA input); in the stabilizing case the rank is at most quadratic in the number of states.
Part III, added in batch 66, decides regularity, inclusion and equality of the full closures of
context-free seeds, classifies the finitely stabilizing sparse one-marker seeds into regular, linear
non-regular and non-context-free closures, and shows that thresholding the exact stabilization
depth is coNP-complete for succinct ray input. Part IV, added in batch 71, shows that the
quadratic order is attained for NFAs: the largest finite rank F (s) of a binary language recognized
by an s-state NFA satisfies (s − 3)2 ≤ F (s) ≤ (2s + 5)2 for s ≥ 4, the lower bound replacing
Part II’s linear one. Parts V–VII, added in batch 73, answer the questions Parts II and IV
left. Part V: on every live residue the largest rank of a length-n hull word is σr n + O(1) with
a rational, computable slope σr , and F (s) ≤ 2(s − 1)2 + 3. Part VI: finite stabilization of an
explicit complete binary DFA is coNP-complete, and the largest finite rank of an s-state complete
binary DFA satisfies (s − 7)2 + 2 ≤ FDFA (s) ≤ s2 + 3 for s ≥ 8. Part VII: F (s) ≤ s2 + 8s for
binary NFAs (s2 + max(8, 2q − 4)s over q letters), so F (s)/s2 → 1 and FDFA (s)/s2 → 1, with an
exact algorithm and F (1), . . . , F (4) = 1, 2, 5, 7, the last three computer-assisted. The exact values
beyond four states and the lower-order terms stay open. Lemmas the new Parts re-prove are cited,
not reprinted, and two lemmas shared by Parts VI and VII are printed once.

automata-and-formal-languages/mix-grammar-cut-sensitive/mix_grammar_report.pdf

says so at the front. What it does contain, with complete written proofs, is a cut-sensitive

and an exhaustive computation through length 21.
34. Natural densities of the Tribonacci word’s additive complexities
automata-and-formal-languages/tribonacci-additive-complexity/article.pdf
from tribonacci_additive_density_research.zip
Answers Remark 19 of Popoli, Shallit and Stipulanti (arXiv:2410.02409). With β the Tribonacci
constant, the additive complexity of the Tribonacci word takes values in {3, 4, 5} with natural
densities d3 = (21β 2 − 38β + 5)/22, d4 = (−55β 2 + 72β + 67)/22 and d5 = (17β 2 − 17β − 25)/11, all
positive and summing to 1, with a power-saving error O(N θ ), θ = log(7/5)/ log β, at every integer
cutoff rather than only along Tribonacci cutoffs. The 76-state output automaton is reconstructed
independently rather than copied, and certified by evaluating its degree-76 characteristic polynomial
at the adjacency matrix.
35. Accessible and strongly connected deterministic automata, to every fixed order
automata-and-formal-languages/accessible-and-strong-automata/article.pdf
from batch 77:   accessible-automata-reproducibility.zip + strong-automata-reproducibility.zip
For a fixed alphabet of k ≥ 2 letters, with t = k(1 − e−t ), ρ = e−t , v = t/k and c = 1 − kρ, Part I
proves that the number an of rooted accessible complete deterministic transition structures on n
states (OEIS A006689, A006690) satisfies an /S(kn + 1, n) = j≤J cj n−j + O(n−J−1 ) for every
                                                                  P

J, with c0 = c, c1 = kρv/(2c) and the small-state counts entering first at ck . The route is an
exact first-failure renewal with two boundaries and a finite coefficient algorithm; c1 , c2 , c3 are
tabulated. Part II adapts the method, with a strict checkpoint, to strongly connected structures
(A027834/A027835, A006691/A006692, A304312/A304313), transfers the expansion through the
classical formal logarithm, confirms the identification that the A304312/A304313 records leave
conjectural, and gives exact cyclic automorphism sectors with a uniform divisor-tail bound and a
self-contained proof of Liskovets’s existing unrooted formula. The leading equivalents (Korshunov,
Bassino–Nicaud, Lebensztayn) are classical, and priority of the refinement is left unresolved:
Korshunov’s 1978 survey was not obtained. The Lambert-W inverses and threshold brackets are
instances of the transseries volume. Merged from two manuscripts; the passages Part II repeats
are printed once, in Part I. Minimal DFAs of finite languages, a different object, are counted in
the report a082161-airy-amplitudes.
36. Binary two-way automata and corank budgets in Brauer monoids
automata-and-formal-languages/binary-two-way-corank/article.pdf
from ProveIt_Binary_Two_Way_Corank.zip (arrival 964621dd2, 7 October 2026, outside the numbered
batches)
For every h ≥ 2 an explicit language Bh over {0, 1}, recognized by a (6h + 2)-state 1NFA, such
that every s-state 2DFA for it satisfies 8s + 2 ≥ 2(5/2)⌊(h−2)/9⌋ ; hence an n-state binary 1NFA
(n ≥ 14) can need at least 14 (5/2)⌊(n−14)/54⌋ − 41 2DFA states. The ingredients are a repetition-
independent corank budget for words in Brauer idempotents, used twice in a relation-addition
amplifier (p = q = 5 optimal for it), a symbol-local sparse-slot matching representation of an
s-state 2DFA in degree at most 2(a + 2)s + 2, and a two-register compiler with a binary decoder;
also a sharp support bound for idempotents with deficit, an integer recurrence for the bound and
eight research questions. Its binary nondeterministic complementation bound is conditional on
an unreproved premise of a companion OpenAI manuscript; the relation-corner and tree-contour
architecture is adapted, with attribution, from an external openai/math release, whose Apache-2.0
licence and third-party notices are staged as the report’s nested licence. Written as a single-source
report; its independent check confirmed the headline bound, rebuilt the sparse-slot representation
(Theorem 3.1) from the text alone, and added two precisions on the trust boundary (Lemma 5.2 is
the companion’s lemma, only outlined here; the exact dependency set of Theorem 1.1). Unrefereed;
not formalized.

40. Shifted Catalan Hankel Polynomials
hankel-determinants/catalan-and-ballot/shifted-catalan-hankel-polynomials/article.pdf
from catalan_hankel_conjecture_solution.zip; batch 39: ProveIt_Catalan_Root_Collisions.zip;

      (m)
For DN (a, b) = det(aCi+j+m + bCi+j+m+1 ), proves the denominator-exponent assertion on page 4
of Barry, arXiv:2011.10827v1, and shows the separately printed Conjecture 2 needs an explicit
one-unit shift correction. The minimal characteristic polynomial is (X 2 −(a+2b)X +b2 )m(m−1)/2+1 ,
so the minimal order is m(m − 1) + 2; all exceptional reduced denominators, the exact coefficient
formula, numerator degree and reciprocal symmetry are supplied. Part II, added in batch 39,
replaces aC + bC ′ by an arbitrary polynomial multiplier and computes the root collisions: exact
sector degrees with nonzero leading constants, the minimal recurrence order off resonance, a
secondary-Hankel factorization and a certified root-free algorithm for the recurrence order. Part III,
added in batch 42, gives every characteristic multiplicity for one repeated root at a cyclotomic
resonance, and shows that the question it answers needed one more parameter: the sign of z L , not
only t and ord(z 2 ). Part IV, added in batch 53, answers the endpoint half of Part II’s question
on uniform limits: for fixed-size clusters of roots approaching the endpoints 0 and 4 (d roots in
all) the normalized determinant is a convergent series in even powers of 1/(N + d/2), locally
uniform through every collision inside a cluster, with a factorized leading profile, an explicit first
interaction term, and ratio to the fixed-endpoint determinant tending to 1 exactly when the
outward displacements are o(N −2 ). Part V, added in batch 55, answers the other half: for fixed-size


                                                 19
clusters of roots colliding on the scale 1/N at fixed bases other than 0 and 4, the determinant is
an exact finite sum of exponential sectors whose amplitudes are convergent series in 1/(N + d/2),
uniform through every collision, with an explicit first correction (this expansion is not even), an
explicit remainder bound, and factorized amplitudes for several separated clusters. For two equal-
multiplicity roots inside the spectrum the limit is an oscillatory Jacobi Hankel determinant, the
first correction oscillates as sin(2(N + t)θ), and simple zeros move by a convergent phase-dependent
expansion. Limits uniform up to the endpoints remain open. Part VI, added in batch 57, lets
                                                                       (m)        (m)
the shift grow with the determinant size: for real a ≥ 0 the ratio DN (a, 1)/DN (0, 1) tends to 1
exactly when aN (N + m + 1)/(4m + 2) → 0, at any relative growth of N and m; the size-biased
inverse Jacobi zeros follow a shifted Marchenko–Pastur (Catalan-moment) law, and three critical
scales, an algebraic free energy and a uniform saddle prefactor describe the crossover.

6.3 Valuations, divisibility and periodicity

54. Binary Carries and the Divisibility of Two-Stack-Sortable Permutation Numbers
congruences-and-valuations/a000139-binary-carries-parity/research.pdf
from a000139_carries_research.zip
Proves Peter Bala’s parity conjecture for OEIS A000139, a(n) = 2(3n)!/((n + 1)!(2n + 1)!): a(n)
is odd exactly when n is odd and its binary expansion contains no 11. The full valuation is
v2 (a(n)) = s(n) + s(n + 1) − s(3n) in binary digit sums. Two independent parity proofs, plus
exact valuation distributions, recurrences, asymptotics, extremal formulas and limit laws.
55. Polynomial divisibility of height-one factorial ratios

A general rational-root criterion for uniform polynomial divisibility of balanced integral factorial

explicit multipliers for every product length, with the least multipliers certified in eight particular
examples. Includes an atlas of the 52 sporadic height-one ratios with recurrences, generating
functions and asymptotic expansions. Part II, added in batch 74, proves integrality and algebraic
generating functions for A347854–A347858 through a rational-dilation theorem for height-one
factorial ratios with composite denominators, settling the Gamma-interpolated values Part I
left open for these five entries; it also proves 6(6n + 1)(12n − 1) | A295432(n) and gives finite-
monodromy certificates and moment laws. Its re-proof of Part I’s A295431 theorem, with larger
constants and no optimality, is printed only as a marked second route.
56. Integrality of the Brent–Glasser–Guttmann asymptotic coefficients
congruences-and-valuations/a321941-asymptotic-coefficient-integrality/article.pdf
from batch 73: A321941_Arithmetic_Conjectures.zip; batch 86:     oeis_research_bundle.zip (its
Part I; its Part II is Part V of a088714-bell-scale-growth)
Brent, Glasser and Guttmann introduced rational coefficients dk in the all-orders asymptotic
expansion of a Hadamard product involving the exponential integral,   conjectured that rk = 64k dk
                                                            2k
(OEIS A321941) is always an integer, and observed rk ≡ k (mod 32) for k ≥ 3. Proves both,
by keeping a quadratic invariant of the underlying second-order recurrence rather than only its
symmetric-square recurrence, and extends them to polynomials Pk (t) ∈ Z[t] with Pk (1) = rk ,
even for k > 0, with a coefficientwise congruence modulo 32. Consequences: the scale 64 is
minimal, denominator bounds are sharp at infinitely many indices and exact when the index has
at most four binary ones, and the inverse expansion has no denominator primes other than 3.
The analytic expansion itself is the published theorem. Part II, added in batch 86 from part of
a two-subject manuscript, proves the negativity conjecture rk < 0 for every k ≥ 3 through an
exact radial-oscillator heat kernel, with a majorant bound for k ≥ 32 and an exact certificate
below; shows that Part I’s constant edge Pk (0) is the leading scale, rk ∼ −16k (k!)2 /(π 3/2 k 3/2 ),
with a complete expansion in 1/k; and finds a logarithmic singularity at 16 of the order-two Borel
transform dk sk /(2k)!, radially only, so its continuation stays open.

7 Tetration and digit stabilization

58. A Counterexample to a Decimal-Length Bound for Tetration Stabilization
tetration-and-digit-stabilization/congruence-speed-decimal-length-bound/article.pdf
from tetration_counterexample.zip
An explicit 2544-digit base A = 1 + qu (q = 52547 ) for which ν10 (Tn+1 − Tn ) = min(1 +
2548n, 2547(n + 1)) at every height, so the congruence speed at height len(A) + 2 is 2548
while the permanent value is 2547. This contradicts the decimal-length form of Conjecture 1 of
Marco Ripà, The congruence speed formula (NNTDM 27(4), 2021). The report audits the later
Ripà–Onnis confirmation: their weaker valuation bound is compatible with the counterexample,
the decimal-length bound is not.
59. Tetration, perfect powers, and frozen decimal digits
tetration-and-digit-stabilization/perfect-power-minima-for-frozen-digits/article.pdf
from tetration_perfect_power_minima.zip
Determines ρ(n), the smallest nth perfect power xn > 1 whose eventual number of newly frozen
terminal decimal digits is exactly n: ρ(1) = 2, ρ(2) = 49, and ρ(n) = B(s)n for n ≥ 3 with
s = n − v2 (n) and B(s) ∈ {2s ± 1, 3 · 2s ± 1} according to s mod 4. All other roots are ruled out
by a deterministic quadratic size barrier plus small exact certificates.
